4.3
Connecting everything together

We summarize the above in the following theorem.

Theorem 1
.

For any integer
r\geq 2
let
k\in\{1,2,\dots,r+1\}
. Consider three vectors
{\bf m}=[{\bf m}_{0},{\bf m}_{1},{\bf m}_{2},...,{\bf m}_{r},{\bf m}_{r+1}]
,
{\bf p}=[{\bf p}_{0},{\bf p}_{1},...,{\bf p}_{r},{\bf p}_{r+1}]
, and
{\bf q}=[{\bf q}_{0},{\bf q}_{1},{\bf q}_{2},\dots,{\bf q}_{r},{\bf q}_{r+1}]
such that
{\bf m}_{0}=1
,
{\bf m}_{r+1}=0
,
1\geq{\bf p}_{0}\geq{\bf p}_{1}\geq{\bf p}_{2}\geq\dots\geq{\bf p}_{r}\geq{\bf p}_{r+1}=0
, and
1\geq{\bf q}_{0}\geq{\bf q}_{1}\geq{\bf q}_{2}\geq\dots\geq{\bf q}_{r}\geq{\bf q}_{r+1}=0
. Let the variances of the independent zero-mean normal elements of
G\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times n}
,
u^{(4,k)}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
{\bf u}^{(2,k)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
, and
{\bf h}^{(k)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be
1
,
{\bf p}_{k-1}{\bf q}_{k-1}-{\bf p}_{k}{\bf q}_{k}
,
{\bf p}_{k-1}-{\bf p}_{k}
,
{\bf q}_{k-1}-{\bf q}_{k}
, respectively. Also, let sets
{\mathcal{X}}
,
\bar{{\mathcal{X}}}
,
{\mathcal{Y}}
, scalars
\beta
,
p
,
s
, and function
f_{\bar{{\bf x}}^{(i_{3})}}(\cdot)
be as in Proposition
1
. For
{\mathcal{U}}_{k}\triangleq[u^{(4,k)},{\bf u}^{(2,k)},{\bf h}^{(2k)}]
we consider the following

\psi(t)={\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\frac{1}{p|s|\sqrt{n}{\bf m}_{r}}\log\left({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{r}}\left(\dots\left({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{2}}\left(\left(\sum_{i_{3}=1}^{l}\left({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}Z_{i_{3}}^{{\bf m}_{1}}\right)^{p}\right)^{\frac{{\bf m}_{2}}{{\bf m}_{1}}}\right)\right)^{\frac{{\bf m}_{3}}{{\bf m}_{2}}}\dots\right)^{\frac{{\bf m}_{r}}{{\bf m}_{r-1}}}\right),

(266)

where

\displaystyle Z_{i_{3}}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{l}\left(\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{l}e^{\beta D_{0}^{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})}}\right)^{s}

\displaystyle D_{0}^{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}({\bf y}^{(i_{2})})^{T}G{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}+\sqrt{1-t}\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(i_{2})})^{T}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{r+1}{\bf u}^{(2,k)}\right)

\displaystyle+\sqrt{t}\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{r+1}u^{(4,k)}\right)+\sqrt{1-t}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{r+1}{\bf h}^{(k)}\right)^{T}{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}+f_{\bar{{\bf x}}^{(i_{3})}}({\bf x}^{(i_{1})}).

For
\zeta_{k}
defined in (
230
) and (
231
) set

\displaystyle\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}^{(i_{3})}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}\frac{Z_{i_{3}}^{{\bf m}_{1}}}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}Z_{i_{3}}^{{\bf m}_{1}}}

\displaystyle\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{k}}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{k}}\frac{\zeta_{k-1}^{\frac{{\bf m}_{k}}{{\bf m}_{k-1}}}}{\zeta_{k}},k\geq 2

\displaystyle\gamma_{00}(i_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\left({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}Z_{i_{3}}^{{\bf m}_{1}}\right)^{p}}{\sum_{i_{3}=1}^{l}\left({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}Z_{i_{3}}^{{\bf m}_{1}}\right)^{p}}

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}(i_{1},i_{2};i_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(C_{i_{3}}^{(i_{1})})^{s}}{Z_{i_{3}}}\frac{A_{i_{3}}^{(i_{1},i_{2})}}{C_{i_{3}}^{(i_{1})}}

\displaystyle\gamma_{01}^{(r)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{k=r}^{2}\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{k}}\gamma_{00}(i_{3})\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}^{(i_{3})}(\gamma_{0}(i_{1},i_{2};i_{3}))

\displaystyle\gamma_{02}^{(r)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{k=r}^{2}\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{k}}\gamma_{00}(i_{3})\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}^{(i_{3})}(\gamma_{0}(i_{1},i_{2};i_{3})\times\gamma_{0}(i_{1},p_{2};i_{3}))

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}^{(r)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{k=r}^{2}\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{k}}\gamma_{00}(i_{3})\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}^{(i_{3})}(\gamma_{0}(i_{1},i_{2};i_{3})\times\gamma_{0}(p_{1},p_{2};i_{3}))

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}^{(r)}\triangleq\gamma_{21}^{(r)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{k=r}^{2}\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{k}}\left(\gamma_{00}(i_{3})\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}^{(i_{3})}\gamma_{0}(i_{1},i_{2};i_{3})\times\gamma_{00}(p_{3})\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}^{(p_{3})}\gamma_{0}(p_{1},p_{2};p_{3})\right)

\displaystyle\gamma_{22}^{(r)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{k=r}^{2}\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{k}}\left(\gamma_{00}(i_{3})\left(\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}^{(i_{3})}\gamma_{0}(i_{1},i_{2};i_{3})\times\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}^{(i_{3})}\gamma_{0}(p_{1},p_{2};i_{3})\right)\right)

\displaystyle\gamma_{k_{1}+1}^{(r)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{k=r}^{k_{1}+1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{k}}\left(\prod_{k=k_{1}}^{2}\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{k}}\gamma_{00}(i_{3})\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}^{(i_{3})}\gamma_{0}(i_{1},i_{2};i_{3})\times\prod_{k=k_{1}}^{2}\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{k}}\gamma_{00}(p_{3})\Phi_{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}^{(p_{3})}\gamma_{0}(p_{1},p_{2};p_{3})\right),k_{1}\geq 2.

For
k_{1}\geq 1
and
\omega(x;p)
as in (
264
), let

\displaystyle\phi_{k_{1}}^{(r)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-s({\bf m}_{k_{1}-1}-{\bf m}_{k_{1}})\omega(k_{1};p)

\displaystyle\times{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle({\bf p}_{k_{1}-1}\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}-({\bf x}^{(p_{1})})^{T}{\bf x}^{(i_{1})})({\bf q}_{k_{1}-1}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\|{\bf y}^{(p_{2})}\|_{2}-({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})})\rangle_{\gamma_{k_{1}}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle\phi_{22}^{(r)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s{\bf m}_{1}(p-1)

\displaystyle\times{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle({\bf p}_{k_{1}-1}\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}-({\bf x}^{(p_{1})})^{T}{\bf x}^{(i_{1})})({\bf q}_{k_{1}-1}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\|{\bf y}^{(p_{2})}\|_{2}-({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})})\rangle_{\gamma_{22}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle\phi_{01}^{(r)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-{\bf p}_{0})(1-{\bf q}_{0}){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}^{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{01}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle\phi_{02}^{(r)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(s-1)(1-{\bf p}_{0}){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\left\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\left({\bf q}_{0}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\|{\bf y}^{(p_{2})}\|_{2}-({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\right)\right\rangle_{\gamma_{02}^{(r)}}.

(269)

Then

\displaystyle\frac{d\psi(t)}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mbox{sign}(s)\beta^{2}}{2\sqrt{n}}\left(\left(\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{r+1}\phi_{k_{1}}^{(r)}\right)+\phi_{22}^{(r)}+\phi_{01}^{(r)}+\phi_{02}^{(r)}\right).

(270)

For a particular choice
{\bf p}_{0}={\bf q}_{0}=1
one additionally has

\displaystyle\frac{d\psi(t)}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mbox{sign}(s)\beta^{2}}{2\sqrt{n}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{r+1}\phi_{k_{1}}^{(r)}+\phi_{22}^{(r)}\right).

(271)

Proof.

The
r=2
scenario follows by results presented in Proposition
2
. The
r\geq 3
scenario
follows via a combination of (
235
)-(
4
), (
250
)-(
252
), (
265
), after one performs summing and canceling scaled terms while additionally noting that, what was shown in (
127
), (
129
), and (
3.1.1
) for the second level in an analogous manner holds for the
r
-th level as well. In particular, one has the following analogue of (
127
)

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{l}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{l}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta_{i_{1}}\frac{T_{1,1,j}}{\sqrt{1-t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta^{2}({\bf p}_{0}-{\bf p}_{1})\Bigg(\Bigg.{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}^{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{01}^{(r)}}

(272)

\displaystyle+(s-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{02}^{(r)}}\Bigg.\Bigg)

\displaystyle-({\bf p}_{0}-{\bf p}_{1})s\beta^{2}(1-{\bf m}_{1}){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{1}^{(r)}},

the following analogue of (
129
)

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{l}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{l}\beta_{i_{1}}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\frac{T_{1,2}}{\sqrt{1-t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta^{2}({\bf q}_{0}-{\bf q}_{1})\Bigg(\Bigg.{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}^{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{01}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle+(s-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\|{\bf y}^{(p_{2})}\|_{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{02}^{(r)}}\Bigg.\Bigg)

\displaystyle-({\bf q}_{0}-{\bf q}_{1})s\beta^{2}(1-{\bf m}_{1}){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle({\bf x}^{(p_{1})})^{T}{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\|{\bf y}^{(p_{2})}\|_{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{1}^{(r)}},

and the following analogue of (
3.1.1
)

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{l}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{l}\beta_{i_{1}}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\frac{T_{1,3}}{\sqrt{t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta^{2}({\bf p}_{0}{\bf q}_{0}-{\bf p}_{1}{\bf q}_{1})\Bigg(\Bigg.{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}^{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{01}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle+(s-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\|{\bf y}^{(p_{2})}\|_{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{02}^{(r)}}\Bigg.\Bigg)

\displaystyle-({\bf p}_{0}{\bf q}_{0}-{\bf p}_{1}{\bf q}_{1})s\beta^{2}(1-{\bf m}_{1}){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{`})}\|_{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\|{\bf y}^{(p_{2})}\|_{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{1}^{(r)}}.

The summing and canceling out procedure works out in the same way for all three sequences,
\left(T_{k,1,j}\right)_{k=1:r+1}
,
\left(T_{k,2}\right)_{k=1:r+1}
, and
\left(T_{k,3}\right)_{k=1:r+1}
. To avoid showing the same mechanism repetitively, we present in details how it works for the first sequence. By trivial
adjustments of the results from the first part of Section
3.1.2
we have the following
r
level analogue of (
142
)

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{l}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{l}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta_{i_{1}}\frac{T_{2,1,j}}{\sqrt{1-t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\bf p}_{1}-{\bf p}_{2})\beta^{2}

(275)

\displaystyle\times\left({\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}^{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{01}^{(r)}}+(s-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{02}^{(r)}}\right)

\displaystyle-({\bf p}_{1}-{\bf p}_{2})s\beta^{2}(1-{\bf m}_{1}){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{1}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle-s\beta^{2}({\bf p}_{1}-{\bf p}_{2})({\bf m}_{1}-{\bf m}_{2})p{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{21}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle+s\beta^{2}({\bf p}_{1}-{\bf p}_{2}){\bf m}_{1}(p-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{22}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\bf p}_{1}-{\bf p}_{2})\beta^{2}

\displaystyle\times\Bigg.\Bigg(\left({\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}^{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{01}^{(r)}}+(s-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{02}^{(r)}}\right)

\displaystyle-s\sum_{v=1}^{2}({\bf m}_{v-1}-{\bf m}_{v})\omega(v;p){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{v}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle+s{\bf m}_{1}(p-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{22}^{(r)}}\Bigg.\Bigg).

To generalize, we assume for
k_{1}\geq 2

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{l}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{l}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta_{i_{1}}\frac{T_{k_{1},1,j}}{\sqrt{1-t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta^{2}({\bf p}_{k_{1}-1}-{\bf p}_{k_{1}})\Bigg(\Bigg.{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}^{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{01}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle+(s-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{02}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle-s\sum_{v=1}^{k_{1}}\left({\bf m}_{v-1}-{\bf m}_{v}\right)\omega(v;p){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\left\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{v}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle+s{\bf m}_{1}(p-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{22}^{(r)}}\Bigg.\Bigg),

and utilizing (
250
) find

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{l}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{l}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta_{i_{1}}\frac{T_{k_{1}+1,1,j}}{\sqrt{1-t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{{\bf p}_{k_{1}}-{\bf p}_{k_{1}+1}}{{\bf p}_{k_{1}-1}-{\bf p}_{k_{1}}}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{l}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{l}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta_{i_{1}}\frac{T_{k_{1},1,j}}{\sqrt{1-t}}

\displaystyle-\Bigg(\Bigg.s\beta^{2}({\bf p}_{k_{1}}-{\bf p}_{k_{1}+1})\left({\bf m}_{k_{1}}-{\bf m}_{k_{1}+1}\right)\omega(k_{1};p)

\displaystyle\times{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\left\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{k_{1}+1}^{(r)}}\Bigg.\Bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{{\bf p}_{k_{1}}-{\bf p}_{k_{1}+1}}{{\bf p}_{k_{1}-1}-{\bf p}_{k_{1}}}\beta^{2}({\bf p}_{k_{1}-1}-{\bf p}_{k_{1}})\Bigg(\Bigg.{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}^{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{01}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle+(s-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{02}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle-s\sum_{v=1}^{k_{1}}\left({\bf m}_{v-1}-{\bf m}_{v}\right)\omega(v;p)

\displaystyle\times{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\left\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{v}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle+s{\bf m}_{1}(p-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{22}^{(r)}}\Bigg.\Bigg)

\displaystyle-\Bigg(\Bigg.s\beta^{2}({\bf p}_{k_{1}}-{\bf p}_{k_{1}+1})\left({\bf m}_{k_{1}}-{\bf m}_{k_{1}+1}\right)\omega(k_{1};p)

\displaystyle\times{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\left\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{k_{1}+1}^{(r)}}\Bigg.\Bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta^{2}({\bf p}_{k_{1}}-{\bf p}_{k_{1}+1})\Bigg(\Bigg.{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}^{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{01}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle+(s-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{02}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle-s\sum_{v=1}^{k_{1}+1}\left({\bf m}_{v-1}-{\bf m}_{v}\right)\omega(v;p){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\left\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{v}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle+s{\bf m}_{1}(p-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{22}^{(r)}}\Bigg.\Bigg).

Since (
4.3
) holds for
k_{1}=2
, (
4.3
) ensures that it actually holds for any
k_{1}\in\{1,2,\dots,r+1\}
. Finally summing over
k_{1}
and utilizing
[
104
]
’s (249) we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{r+1}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{l}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{l}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta_{i_{1}}\frac{T_{k_{1},1,j}}{\sqrt{1-t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta^{2}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{r+1}({\bf p}_{k_{1}-1}-{\bf p}_{k_{1}})\Bigg(\Bigg.{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}^{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{01}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle+(s-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{02}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle-s\sum_{v=1}^{k_{1}}\left({\bf m}_{v-1}-{\bf m}_{v}\right)\omega(v;p){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\left\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{k_{1}}^{(r)}}\Bigg.\Bigg)

\displaystyle+s\sum_{k_{1}=2}^{r+1}({\bf p}_{k_{1}-1}-{\bf p}_{k_{1}}){\bf m}_{1}(p-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{22}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta^{2}({\bf p}_{0}-{\bf p}_{r+1})\Bigg(\Bigg.{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}^{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{01}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle+(s-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{02}^{(r)}}\Bigg.\Bigg)

\displaystyle-s\beta^{2}\sum_{v=1}^{r+1}{\bf p}_{v-1}\left({\bf m}_{v-1}-{\bf m}_{v}\right)\omega(v;p){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\left\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{v}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle+s\beta^{2}{\bf p}_{1}{\bf m}_{1}(p-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{22}^{(r)}}.

We then analogously find for the other two sequences

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{r+1}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{l}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{l}\beta_{i_{1}}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\frac{T_{k_{1},2}}{\sqrt{1-t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta^{2}({\bf q}_{0}-{\bf q}_{r+1})\Bigg(\Bigg.{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}^{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{01}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle+(s-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\|{\bf y}^{(p_{2})}\|_{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{02}^{(r)}}\Bigg.\Bigg)

\displaystyle-s\beta^{2}\sum_{v=1}^{r+1}{\bf q}_{v-1}\left({\bf m}_{v-1}-{\bf m}_{v}\right)\omega(v;p)

\displaystyle\times{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\left\langle\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\|{\bf y}^{(p_{2})}\|_{2}({\bf x}^{(p_{1})})^{T}{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{v}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle+s\beta^{2}{\bf p}_{1}{\bf m}_{1}(p-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{22}^{(r)}},

and

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{r+1}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{l}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{l}\beta_{i_{1}}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\frac{T_{k_{1},3}}{\sqrt{t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta^{2}({\bf p}_{0}{\bf q}_{0}-{\bf p}_{r+1}{\bf q}_{r+1})\Bigg(\Bigg.{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}^{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{01}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle+(s-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\|{\bf y}^{(p_{2})}\|_{2}\rangle_{\gamma_{02}^{(r)}}\Bigg.\Bigg)

\displaystyle-s\sum_{v=1}^{r+1}{\bf p}_{v-1}{\bf q}_{v-1}\left({\bf m}_{v-1}-{\bf m}_{v}\right)\omega(v;p)

\displaystyle\times{\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\left\langle\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\|{\bf y}^{(p_{2})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{v}^{(r)}}

\displaystyle+s\beta^{2}{\bf p}_{1}{\bf m}_{1}(p-1){\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\langle\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf x}^{(p_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(p_{2})})^{T}{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\rangle_{\gamma_{22}^{(r)}},

Combining (
235
)-(
4
), (
265
), and (
4.3
)-(
4.3
) one obtains that (
1
) and (
270
) indeed hold.
∎

5
Practical relevance

As noted in
[
104
]
, considered models and associated bilinearly indexed random processes relate to a host of well known random structures and accompanying classical optimization problems. These include both feasibility and standard optimal objective seeking ones. The key features associated with such problems (optimal objective, critical dimensionality, etc.) are usually associated with the so-called
typical
behavior and the concepts from
[
104
]
are powerful enough to handle them. On the other hand, in recent years features associated with
atypical
behavior gained a lot of interest as they are presumed to be tightly connected to the appearance of the so called
computational gap
– a phenomenon where known practically feasible algorithms fail to achieve the predicted theoretical limit. The machinery introduced here presents an upgrade on
[
104
]
that
is powerful enough to allow for studying a much wider class of random structures features including algorithmically the most sought after atypical ones.

To see how this connection materializes, we first recall that the key object of interest from Theorem
1
is the following function

\psi(t)={\mathbb{E}}_{G,{\mathcal{U}}_{r+1}}\frac{1}{p|s|\sqrt{n}{\bf m}_{r}}\log\left({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{r}}\left(\dots\left({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{2}}\left(\left(\sum_{i_{3}=1}^{l}\left({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}Z^{{\bf m}_{1}}\right)^{p}\right)^{\frac{{\bf m}_{2}}{{\bf m}_{1}}}\right)\right)^{\frac{{\bf m}_{3}}{{\bf m}_{2}}}\dots\right)^{\frac{{\bf m}_{r}}{{\bf m}_{r-1}}}\right),

(281)

where

\displaystyle Z_{i_{3}}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{l}\left(\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{l}e^{\beta D_{0}^{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})}}\right)^{s}

\displaystyle D_{0}^{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}({\bf y}^{(i_{2})})^{T}G{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}+\sqrt{1-t}\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}({\bf y}^{(i_{2})})^{T}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{r+1}{\bf u}^{(2,k)}\right)

\displaystyle+\sqrt{t}\|{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\|_{2}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{r+1}u^{(4,k)}\right)+\sqrt{1-t}\|{\bf y}^{(i_{2})}\|_{2}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{r+1}{\bf h}^{(k)}\right)^{T}{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}+f_{{\bf x}^{(i_{3})}}({\bf x}^{(i_{1})}),

and

{\mathcal{X}}=\{{\bf x}^{(1)},{\bf x}^{(2)},\dots,{\bf x}^{(l)}\}
with
{\bf x}^{(i)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},1\leq i\leq l
and
{\mathcal{Y}}=\{{\bf y}^{(1)},{\bf y}^{(2)},\dots,{\bf y}^{(l)}\}
with
{\bf y}^{(i)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m},1\leq i\leq l
. The so-called linear regime,
\frac{m}{n}=\alpha
, where
\alpha
remains constant as
n\rightarrow\infty
is of prevalent interest.

As
[
104
]
demonstrated, classical neural network perceptron concepts can be cast as feasibility problems and fit the model studied here. For example, for the so-called spherical perceptron one takes
s=-1
,
{\mathcal{X}}={\mathbb{S}}^{n}
,
\bar{{\mathcal{X}}}=\emptyset
,
{\mathcal{Y}}={\mathbb{S}}_{+}^{n}
(where
{\mathbb{S}}_{+}^{m}
is the unit sphere positive orthant portion), and
p=1
and then observes that
\psi(\cdot)
in (
281
) is properly adjusted associated free energy (see, e.g.,
[
92
,
99
,
98
,
48
,
47
,
81
,
82
]
).
As is the case in general random optimizations, two values of this function are often of key relevance (see, e.g.,
[
35
,
33
,
34
,
36
,
37
,
7
,
92
,
99
,
98
,
48
,
47
,
80
,
27
,
115
,
114
,
113
,
26
,
59
,
19
,
110
,
81
,
82
]
). In particular, we have

\lim_{n,\beta\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\psi\left(1\right)}{\beta}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{G}\frac{\min_{{\bf x}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{m}}\max_{{\bf y}\in{\mathbb{S}}_{+}^{m}}{\bf y}^{T}G{\bf x}}{\sqrt{n}}

(283)

and
\lim_{n,\beta\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\psi\left(0\right)}{\beta}
which are precisely the associated ground state energy and its intended (presumably analytically simpler) decoupled counterpart in the thermodynamic limit.

The famous binary perceptron (BP) (see, e.g.,
[
92
,
48
,
47
,
95
,
63
,
51
,
62
,
109
,
71
,
24
,
76
,
116
,
29
,
55
,
103
,
66
]
) fits the considered model in exactly the same way as the spherical does with the only difference being that now
{\mathcal{X}}=\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\}^{n}
. One then analogously has that

\lim_{n,\beta\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\psi\left(1\right)}{\beta}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{G}\frac{\min_{{\bf x}\in\left\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\right\}^{n}}\max_{{\bf y}\in{\mathbb{S}}_{+}^{m}}{\bf y}^{T}G{\bf x}}{\sqrt{n}}

(284)

and
\lim_{n,\beta\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\psi\left(0\right)}{\beta}
are the thermodynamic limit values of the associated ground state energy and its decoupled counterpart (the concentrations actually ensure that
{\mathbb{E}}_{G}
can be removed in (
283
) and (
284
)). Various other perceptron variants including closely related symmetric binary perceptrons (SBP) from
[
11
,
2
,
1
,
18
,
41
,
76
,
10
,
79
,
22
,
8
,
23
]
(or their discrepancy minimization equivalents from, e.g.,
[
61
,
85
,
68
,
42
,
78
,
9
]
) can be fit into the model in an analogous fashion as well.

The machinery of
[
104
]
can be used to determine the critical
\alpha
(the so-called perceptron capacity,
\alpha_{c}
) for which
\psi(1)
transitions from zero to a nonzero value which corresponds to BP transitioning from successful to unsuccessful associative memory. Moreover,
[
104
]
can be used to characterize various other features associated with the so-called typical behavior. For example, when one operates in regimes below the capacity (
\alpha<\alpha_{c}
), there will be a large number of feasibility problem solutions (BP memories) and they are ordered in a typical hierarchical way. The key features of these solutions, their number (entropy) as well as how they relate to each other can be precisely determined through the utilization of
[
104
]
. On the other hand, when one tries to algorithmically find these solutions, an NP problem is faced. Since NP-ness is a
worst case
concept it rarely can give a proper explanation about typical solvability of underlying optimization problems. Things are practically actually even worse. For example, a host of excellent BP algorithms exist that work fairly well for a large portion of
\alpha<\alpha_{c}
region (see, e.g.,
[
25
,
12
,
58
,
62
]
). In concrete terms, the zero threshold BP (precisely the one characterized via (
284
)) has the capacity
\alpha_{c}\approx 0.833
and very good algorithms can be constructed in the
\alpha
-regions up to
\alpha_{alg}\approx 0.75
(sometimes this can be even pushed to
0.77
). It is an extraordinary challenge to determine precisely the critical algorithmic value
\alpha_{alg}
below which the efficient algorithms exist and to characterize the structural phenomenology behind their existence. The fact that the current
\alpha_{alg}
is below the
\alpha_{c}
constitutes the presence of the so-called
computational gap
– a key feature present in a plethora of (random) optimization problems (see, e.g.,
[
69
,
44
,
45
,
46
,
4
,
3
,
43
]
).

An enormous effort has been put forth in studying these concepts over the last 10-15 years in a range of optimization problems that go way beyond the BP. Many great results have been achieved but the generic conclusions and overall resolution remain unreachable. Among the most attractive approaches are studies of clustered solutions. The so-called
overlap gap property
(OGP) approach
[
38
,
44
,
45
,
46
,
3
,
69
]
connects algorithmic efficacy to the existence of pairs (in general
m
-tuples) of solutions at arbitrary Hamming distances. For example, for an analytically presumably more convenient SBP alternative, it is known (see, e.g.,
[
41
,
18
]
or
[
42
]
for the corresponding discrepancy minimization) that OGP is absent for
\alpha
’s well below the critical capacity, indicating potential existence of computational gap (provided that algorithmic tractability is indeed impacted by OGP; for a recent shortest path problem example that disproves OGP generic hardness impact see, e.g.,
[
65
]
(earlier disproving examples included simple algebraically solvable
k
-XOR ones); it should be noted however, that this does not disprove OGP’s relevance when it comes to particular algorithms or potentially its generic hardness value for different optimization problems). While the OGP’s generic role with respect to hardness remains to be determined, its absence precludes many particular classes of algorithms (see, e.g.,
[
41
]
). In fact, the OGP’s relevance is further strengthened for many problems by the existence of practically feasible algorithms in a similar or even the exact
\alpha
range (see, e.g.,
[
77
,
44
,
45
,
40
,
111
]
). Its direct impact on polynomial solvability of famous SK model
[
83
]
might be even more valuable
[
70
]
.

An alternative view to the above OGP was put forth in
[
57
,
56
]
where the entropy of typical solutions was considered. A stronger entropic refinement was then presented in
[
13
,
14
,
18
]
, where the existence of atypical well connected clusters is considered and the concepts related to the so-called
local entropy
(LE) are proposed and discussed. The idea is that while typical solutions might be well apart (basically disconnected from each other and unreachable via simple bit flipping type of local roaming
[
57
,
56
,
76
,
1
]
), there still exist well connected but rather rare (subdominant) clusters. It is then postulated (and in some cases fully justified by running concrete algorithms) that somewhat miraculously efficient algorithms manage to find precisely these rare clusters (for a sampling type of justification in this direction for SBP, see, e.g.,
[
10
]
). This consequently suggested that properties of such clusters might be responsible for generic computational hardness. In particular, behavior of their local entropy (its monotonicity, negativity, or even non-existence) was speculated in
[
13
,
14
,
18
]
to be directly connected to the algorithmic hardness. We should also add that for the SBP,
[
2
]
showed that for sufficiently low
\alpha
rare well-connected clusters of maximal diameter indeed exist. Moreover, existence of such clusters, albeit of linear diameter, was also shown in
[
2
]
even for any
\alpha<\alpha_{c}
. Reconnecting back to the OGP,
[
23
]
showed that LE results for small
\alpha
SBP at least scaling wise to a large degree match the OGP predictions of
[
41
]
(and modulo a log term the algorithmic performance of
[
21
]
). While this establishes a nice OGP – LE correspondence,
providing a definite answer as to whether or not any of these properties indeed critically impacts
generic
algorithmic hardness (and if so then precisely how and why) remains extraordinary challenge. On the other hand, irrespective to their algorithmic hardness relevance, all of these properties provide rather deep insights into the intrinsic organization of random structures and studying them warrants an independent interest as well.

Clearly, all of the above concepts are logically more foundational and analytically much harder than the original studying of the capacity and its phase-transitioning behavior. Consequently, with a few special cases exceptions the analytical progress is often restricted to statistical physics replica methods. The machinery that we introduce here provides a large deviation view of the underlying phenomena and as such can be used for studying atypical features. To see this practically, let us again look at the binary perceptron and for a given configurational overlap
\bar{\delta}
, take
\bar{{\mathcal{X}}}=\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\}^{n}
,
{\mathcal{X}}={\mathcal{X}}^{(i_{3})}=\{{\bf x}|{\bf x}\in\bar{{\mathcal{X}}},\left(\bar{{\bf x}}^{(i_{3})}\right)^{T}{\bf x}=\bar{\delta}\}
, and
f_{\bar{{\bf x}}^{(i_{3})}}=0
. For
h(\cdot)
denoting the standard Heaviside function, one then observes that

\lim_{n,\beta,p\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\psi\left(1\right)}{\sqrt{n}}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\left(\max_{\bar{{\bf x}}^{(i_{3})}}\sum_{{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\in{\mathcal{X}},\left(\bar{{\bf x}}^{(i_{3})}\right)^{T}{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}=\bar{\delta}}\left(1-h\left(\max_{{\bf y}\in{\mathbb{S}}_{+}^{m}}{\bf y}^{T}G{\bf x}^{(i_{1})}\right)\right)\right)}{n}\triangleq\sigma(\bar{\delta})

(285)

and
\lim_{n,\beta\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\psi\left(0\right)}{\beta}
are the thermodynamic limit values of the local entropy (LE),
\sigma(\bar{\delta})
, and its decoupled counterpart (these are the LEs of a general so-called reference configuration; various other highly relevant types of LEs can be considered as well, see, e.g.,
[
13
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
for BP or e.g.,
[
23
]
for SBP). It is not that difficult to see that
\sigma(\bar{\delta})
is effectively the
n
-scaled exponent of the number of solutions in the densest cluster at Hamming distance
\frac{1-\bar{\delta}}{2}
from the cluster’s center. We also note that the constraint free variant of (
285
) can be conveniently obtained by taking
f_{\bar{{\bf x}}^{(i_{3})}}=\nu\left(\left(\bar{{\bf x}}^{(i_{3})}\right)^{T}{\bf x}-\bar{\delta}\right)
, where scalar
\nu
is an optimizing variable (this avenue is pursued in companion paper
[
105
]
in full detail).

The above example is only for illustrative purposes and is selected due to perceptron’s enormous popularity over the last decade primarily motivated by the machine learning and neureal networks applications. However, a rather large collection of closely related relevant examples exists as well. In addition to the feasibility problems (such as perceptrons) it also includes many standard optimal objective seeking optimizations as well. For example, in famous Hopfield models, one is often interested in behavior of the free energy. Usually a single optimal configuration is associated with the ground state energy values (in a similar way how a single typical solution is associated with the
\alpha_{c}
capacity in perceptrons). However, away from the ground state, many other energetic band levels can be achieved by a large set of configurations. Their typical and atypical behavior can then be studied via the concepts introduced here. While the key mathematical engines that each of these studies relies on are already shown here, additional problem specific technical adjustments are often needed as well so that one can obtain practically usable concrete results. For several widely popular problems we discuss these adjustments in separate companion papers.

6
Conclusion

In
[
104
]
a powerful
fully lifted
(fl) probabilistic interpolating mechanism of bilinearly indexed random processes (blirps) was introduced. We here present a large deviation view upgrade. Differently from
[
104
]
, which is intended for studying
typical
features of random structures, the large deviations nature of the machinery introduced here allows to substantially extend the range of applicability. In particular, studying analytically much harder
atypical
features is now possible as well.

To give a bit of a flavor regarding practical applications, we discussed how the presented concepts and underlying models directly relate to studying the so-called
computational gaps
in NP problems. The existence of atypical dense solutions clusters is predicated to play a key role in justifying the success of efficient algorithms. Studying such atypicalities is therefore certainly of great interest for many classes of algorithms but on its own or together with other features might be even universally relevant driving force behind the computational gaps. Due to their enormous popularity over the last decade, perceptrons were chosen as convenient illustrative examples to highlight the connection between the presented mathematical concepts and the relevance of studying atypical random structures’ features. However, the presented machinery is a very powerful generic tool that allows for various further extensions and a range of applications way beyond classical perceptrons. As such extensions/applications are problem specific, we discuss them together with the relevance of the results that they provide in separate papers.
